Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. It’s really the job stuff that’s gotten embarrassing, because a straightforward install can take me way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind and are difficult to get rid of?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s constant, exactly, but there are definitely thoughts that kind of barge in and won’t leave.\n\nLike even after I know I checked a panel or a disconnect, I’ll get this thought of, “What if I missed one loose neutral?” or “What if I left something that could start a fire?” And I’ll try to tell myself I already checked it, that it’s fine, but it can follow me all the way home.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not trying to be difficult or anything—it just gets hard to shake once it’s in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about missing something or causing a fire make sense, or do they seem irrational to you?\n\nPatient: Part of me knows it’s probably more than it needs to be. I mean, I do check the panel or the disconnect, and I’m not careless at work.\n\nBut then the thought barges in anyway—like, what if I missed one loose neutral, or what if I left something that could start a fire? And even if I try to shake it off, it can follow me all the way home.\n\nSo… I guess it doesn’t feel completely rational, but it feels hard to ignore, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Kind of, yeah. I don’t know if it’s a strict “order” exactly, but I feel like I have to go back over the same things until it feels settled enough to leave it alone.

So I’ll check the breakers, the labels, the torque marks, then I’ll look at the photos again… and if something doesn’t feel right, I may start over or keep circling back. Sometimes I even drive back to the site, which I know sounds like a lot.

It’s not that I want to waste time. It’s just that a simple install ends up taking way longer than it should, and, honestly, people are starting to notice.

Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to overstate it, but it does feel like I’m checking so nothing gets missed that could turn into a problem.

I don’t want to overstate it, but it does feel like I’m checking so nothing gets missed that could turn into a problem.\n\nThat’s kind of what brings me in, honestly. I keep going back over jobs—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took—and sometimes I’ll even drive back to the site if I can’t let it go. I know I’m trying to prevent something bad from happening, but it gets to where a simple install takes way longer than it should.\n\nAnd, uh… people are starting to notice, so I figured I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors and thoughts affecting your relationships with family or friends outside of work?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s ruining things or anything… but it does spill over.\n\nMy schedule has been slipping because jobs I should wrap up in one visit turn into me calling back, checking again, looking over pictures, that kind of thing. And that takes time away from the apprentices too, because I’m not as available or I’m tied up going back over something.\n\nOutside of work, I’ve been skipping some stuff I used to do after hours—seeing people, doing things I enjoy—because I’m still reviewing a job in my head or wondering if I need to go back. So… yeah, I guess it’s affecting things more than I’d like to admit. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors and thoughts are significantly interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if I’m being honest, it is starting to.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but my schedule’s been slipping because jobs that should be done in one visit turn into callbacks, extra checks, looking things over again. And that’s starting to affect how confident clients are in me, I think… plus it takes time away from the apprentices when I’m tied up rechecking instead of being available.\n\nAnd after work, I’ve been skipping things I used to enjoy because I’m still stuck reviewing a job in my head or wondering if I need to go back. So… yeah, I’d say it’s interfering more than I’d like. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s… not unbearable all the time, but it’s definitely stressful.

What brings me in is I keep going back over jobs again and again—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took—just trying to be sure I didn’t miss something. And sometimes I’ll even drive back to a site if I can’t shake it.

So the distress is more like… I know it shouldn’t take over the day like that, but it does. A simple install ends up taking way longer than it should, and then I feel embarrassed because people are starting to notice. I don’t know if that’s “significant” enough, but it feels like more than I can keep brushing off.

I mean, I want to do good work, obviously, but a simple install is taking me way longer than it should, and people are starting to notice. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but… that’s what’s been bringing me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating these checking behaviors in other areas of your life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s at work, honestly. I haven’t noticed it causing the same kind of problem at home. It’s really the job stuff that’s gotten embarrassing, because a straightforward install can take me way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind and are difficult to get rid of?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s constant, exactly, but there are definitely thoughts that kind of barge in and won’t leave.\n\nLike even after I know I checked a panel or a disconnect, I’ll get this thought of, “What if I missed one loose neutral?” or “What if I left something that could start a fire?” And I’ll try to tell myself I already checked it, that it’s fine, but it can follow me all the way home.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I don’t think so… not really symmetrical, if that makes sense.

It’s not like things have to line up a certain way or look perfect for the sake of it. I mean, I like my work neat, of course, but I think that’s pretty normal in electrical work. When I’m checking, it’s more about making sure it’s safe and correct, not so much that it feels “just right.”

I’m an electrician, and I keep finding myself going back over jobs again and again—checking breakers, labels, torque marks, photos, things like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll even drive back to a site just to make sure I didn’t miss something. I mean, I want to do good work, obviously, but a simple install is taking me way longer than it should, and people are starting to notice. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but… that’s what’s been bringing me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating these checking behaviors in other areas of your life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s at work, honestly. That’s where it shows up the clearest—like I’ll check the breakers and labels and torque marks, look back through photos, and sometimes even go back to the site if I can’t shake the feeling I missed something.\n\nOutside of work… I mean, not in a way I’d want to make sound dramatic. I haven’t noticed it causing the same kind of problem at home. It’s really the job stuff that’s gotten embarrassing, because a straightforward install can take me way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind and are difficult to get rid of?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s constant, exactly, but there are definitely thoughts that kind of barge in and won’t leave.\n\nLike even after I know I checked a panel or a disconnect, I’ll get this thought of, “What if I missed one loose neutral?” or “What if I left something that could start a fire?” And I’ll try to tell myself I already checked it, that it’s fine, but it can follow me all the way home.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not trying to be difficult or anything—it just gets hard to shake once it’s in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about missing something or causing a fire make sense, or do they seem irrational to you?\n\nPatient: Part of me knows it’s probably more than it needs to be. I mean, I do check the panel or the disconnect, and I’m not careless at work.\n\nBut then the thought barges in anyway—like, what if I missed one loose neutral, or what if I left something that could start a fire? And even if I try to shake it off, it can follow me all the way home.\n\nSo… I guess it doesn’t feel completely rational, but it feels hard to ignore, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah. I don’t know if it’s a strict “order” exactly, but I feel like I have to go back over the same things until it feels settled enough to leave it alone.\n\nSo I’ll check the breakers, the labels, the torque marks, then I’ll look at the photos again… and if something doesn’t feel right, I may start over or keep circling back. Sometimes I even drive back to the site, which I know sounds like a lot.\n\nIt’s not that I want to waste time. It’s just that a simple install ends up taking way longer than it should, and, honestly, people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking things to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to overstate it, but it does feel like I’m checking so nothing gets missed that could turn into a problem.\n\nThat’s kind of what brings me in, honestly. I keep going back over jobs—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took—and sometimes I’ll even drive back to the site if I can’t let it go. I know I’m trying to prevent something bad from happening, but it gets to where a simple install takes way longer than it should.\n\nAnd, uh… people are starting to notice, so I figured I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors and thoughts affecting your relationships with family or friends outside of work?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. So… yeah, I’d say it’s interfering more than I’d like. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts and checking behaviors cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… not unbearable all the time, but it’s definitely stressful.\n\nWhat brings me in is I keep going back over jobs again and again—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took—just trying to be sure I didn’t miss something. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wouldn’t say every aspect… I mean, I don’t want to sound like I’m trying to micromanage everything.

But with my own work, yeah, I do feel like I can’t really relax until I’ve gone back through the important parts again—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’m just being thorough, which… in this line of work isn’t a bad thing. But then it turns into checking the same things more than once, and sometimes even going back to the site if I can’t get comfortable with it.

So maybe not “control every aspect,” exactly, but I do feel like I need enough control over the job to be sure I didn’t miss anything. And lately it’s been taking longer than it should. People are starting to notice that, I think.

I haven’t noticed it causing the same kind of problem at home. It’s really the job stuff that’s gotten embarrassing, because a straightforward install can take me way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind and are difficult to get rid of?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s constant, exactly, but there are definitely thoughts that kind of barge in and won’t leave.\n\nLike even after I know I checked a panel or a disconnect, I’ll get this thought of, “What if I missed one loose neutral?” or “What if I left something that could start a fire?” And I’ll try to tell myself I already checked it, that it’s fine, but it can follow me all the way home.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not trying to be difficult or anything—it just gets hard to shake once it’s in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about missing something or causing a fire make sense, or do they seem irrational to you?\n\nPatient: Part of me knows it’s probably more than it needs to be. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Perfect might be too strong a word… I don’t want to make it sound like I’m impossible to satisfy.

But yeah, I guess in practice I don’t really let myself call it done until I’ve checked the important things again—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took. And sometimes I’ll keep going back over the same stuff because I’m worried I missed something.

It’s embarrassing to say, but a simple install can end up taking way longer than it should. And there have been times I’ve driven back to a site just to be sure. People are starting to notice, I think.

I mean, I want to do good work, obviously, but a simple install is taking me way longer than it should, and people are starting to notice. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but… that’s what’s been bringing me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating these checking behaviors in other areas of your life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s at work, honestly. That’s where it shows up the clearest—like I’ll check the breakers and labels and torque marks, look back through photos, and sometimes even go back to the site if I can’t shake the feeling I missed something.\n\nOutside of work… I mean, not in a way I’d want to make sound dramatic. I haven’t noticed it causing the same kind of problem at home. It’s really the job stuff that’s gotten embarrassing, because a straightforward install can take me way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind and are difficult to get rid of?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s constant, exactly, but there are definitely thoughts that kind of barge in and won’t leave.\n\nLike even after I know I checked a panel or a disconnect, I’ll get this thought of, “What if I missed one loose neutral?” or “What if I left something that could start a fire?” And I’ll try to tell myself I already checked it, that it’s fine, but it can follow me all the way home.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not trying to be difficult or anything—it just gets hard to shake once it’s in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about missing something or causing a fire make sense, or do they seem irrational to you?\n\nPatient: Part of me knows it’s probably more than it needs to be. I mean, I do check the panel or the disconnect, and I’m not careless at work.\n\nBut then the thought barges in anyway—like, what if I missed one loose neutral, or what if I left something that could start a fire? And even if I try to shake it off, it can follow me all the way home.\n\nSo… I guess it doesn’t feel completely rational, but it feels hard to ignore, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah. I don’t know if it’s a strict “order” exactly, but I feel like I have to go back over the same things until it feels settled enough to leave it alone.\n\nSo I’ll check the breakers, the labels, the torque marks, then I’ll look at the photos again… and if something doesn’t feel right, I may start over or keep circling back. Sometimes I even drive back to the site, which I know sounds like a lot.\n\nIt’s not that I want to waste time. It’s just that a simple install ends up taking way longer than it should, and, honestly, people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking things to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to overstate it, but it does feel like I’m checking so nothing gets missed that could turn into a problem.\n\nThat’s kind of what brings me in, honestly. So… yeah, I’d say it’s interfering more than I’d like. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think there is a routine to it, though I don’t know if I’d call it a pattern exactly.

I’ll usually go back through the same things—breakers, labels, torque marks, then the photos I took—and I kind of need to do it in that way before I feel okay saying it’s done. It’s not like a ritual in my head, or at least I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but if I skip something or feel like I rushed it, I’ll start thinking maybe I missed something.

And then I end up checking again… sometimes more than once. A simple install can take a lot longer than it should, and I’ve even driven back to a site before just to make sure. I know that probably sounds excessive. People are starting to notice, I think.

I’m not trying to be difficult or anything—it just gets hard to shake once it’s in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about missing something or causing a fire make sense, or do they seem irrational to you?\n\nPatient: Part of me knows it’s probably more than it needs to be. And there have been times I’ve driven back to a site just to be sure. People are starting to notice, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the way you check things has to follow a specific routine or pattern to feel complete?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there is a routine to it, though I don’t know if I’d call it a pattern exactly.\n\nI’ll usually go back through the same things—breakers, labels, torque marks, then the photos I took—and I kind of need to do it in that way before I feel okay saying it’s done. It’s not like a ritual in my head, or at least I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but if I skip something or feel like I rushed it, I’ll start thinking maybe I missed something.\n\nAnd then I end up checking again… sometimes more than once. A simple install can take a lot longer than it should, and I’ve even driven back to a site before just to make sure. I know that probably sounds excessive. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more about preventing something bad from happening… though I tell myself it’s just making sure everything is right.

With electrical work, if I start wondering whether I missed a breaker label or a torque mark or something in the photos, I get this feeling like I shouldn’t leave it alone yet. So I go back over it again. And sometimes again after that.

I don’t mean that I’m trying to be difficult or waste time. I know being careful is part of the job. But lately it’s gone past normal careful, I think. A simple install can take me way longer than it should, and there have been times I’ve driven back to a site just to check. People are starting to notice, which is part of why I’m here.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s mostly work stuff that’s gotten a little out of hand. I’m an electrician, and I keep finding myself going back over jobs again and again—checking breakers, labels, torque marks, photos, things like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll even drive back to a site just to make sure I didn’t miss something. I mean, I want to do good work, obviously, but a simple install is taking me way longer than it should, and people are starting to notice. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but… that’s what’s been bringing me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating these checking behaviors in other areas of your life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s at work, honestly. That’s where it shows up the clearest—like I’ll check the breakers and labels and torque marks, look back through photos, and sometimes even go back to the site if I can’t shake the feeling I missed something.\n\nOutside of work… I mean, not in a way I’d want to make sound dramatic. I haven’t noticed it causing the same kind of problem at home. It’s really the job stuff that’s gotten embarrassing, because a straightforward install can take me way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind and are difficult to get rid of?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s constant, exactly, but there are definitely thoughts that kind of barge in and won’t leave.\n\nLike even after I know I checked a panel or a disconnect, I’ll get this thought of, “What if I missed one loose neutral?” or “What if I left something that could start a fire?” And I’ll try to tell myself I already checked it, that it’s fine, but it can follow me all the way home.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not trying to be difficult or anything—it just gets hard to shake once it’s in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about missing something or causing a fire make sense, or do they seem irrational to you?\n\nPatient: Part of me knows it’s probably more than it needs to be. I mean, I do check the panel or the disconnect, and I’m not careless at work.\n\nBut then the thought barges in anyway—like, what if I missed one loose neutral, or what if I left something that could start a fire? And even if I try to shake it off, it can follow me all the way home.\n\nSo… I guess it doesn’t feel completely rational, but it feels hard to ignore, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah. I don’t know if it’s a strict “order” exactly, but I feel like I have to go back over the same things until it feels settled enough to leave it alone.\n\nSo I’ll check the breakers, the labels, the torque marks, then I’ll look at the photos again… and if something doesn’t feel right, I may start over or keep circling back. Sometimes I even drive back to the site, which I know sounds like a lot.\n\nIt’s not that I want to waste time. It’s just that a simple install ends up taking way longer than it should, and, honestly, people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking things to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to overstate it, but it does feel like I’m checking so nothing gets missed that could turn into a problem.\n\nThat’s kind of what brings me in, honestly. I keep going back over jobs—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took—and sometimes I’ll even drive back to the site if I can’t let it go. I know I’m trying to prevent something bad from happening, but it gets to where a simple install takes way longer than it should.\n\nAnd, uh… people are starting to notice, so I figured I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors and thoughts affecting your relationships with family or friends outside of work?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s ruining things or anything… but it does spill over.\n\nMy schedule has been slipping because jobs I should wrap up in one visit turn into me calling back, checking again, looking over pictures, that kind of thing. And that takes time away from the apprentices too, because I’m not as available or I’m tied up going back over something.\n\nOutside of work, I’ve been skipping some stuff I used to do after hours—seeing people, doing things I enjoy—because I’m still reviewing a job in my head or wondering if I need to go back. So… yeah, I guess it’s affecting things more than I’d like to admit. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors and thoughts are significantly interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if I’m being honest, it is starting to.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but my schedule’s been slipping because jobs that should be done in one visit turn into callbacks, extra checks, looking things over again. And that’s starting to affect how confident clients are in me, I think… plus it takes time away from the apprentices when I’m tied up rechecking instead of being available.\n\nAnd after work, I’ve been skipping things I used to enjoy because I’m still stuck reviewing a job in my head or wondering if I need to go back. So… yeah, I’d say it’s interfering more than I’d like. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts and checking behaviors cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… not unbearable all the time, but it’s definitely stressful.\n\nWhat brings me in is I keep going back over jobs again and again—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took—just trying to be sure I didn’t miss something. And sometimes I’ll even drive back to a site if I can’t shake it.\n\nSo the distress is more like… I know it shouldn’t take over the day like that, but it does. A simple install ends up taking way longer than it should, and then I feel embarrassed because people are starting to notice. I don’t know if that’s “significant” enough, but it feels like more than I can keep brushing off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be just right or symmetrical when you're checking your work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really symmetrical, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s not like things have to line up a certain way or look perfect for the sake of it. I mean, I like my work neat, of course, but I think that’s pretty normal in electrical work. When I’m checking, it’s more about making sure it’s safe and correct, not so much that it feels “just right.”\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what you mean by that?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to control every aspect of your work to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say every aspect… I mean, I don’t want to sound like I’m trying to micromanage everything.\n\nBut with my own work, yeah, I do feel like I can’t really relax until I’ve gone back through the important parts again—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’m just being thorough, which… in this line of work isn’t a bad thing. But then it turns into checking the same things more than once, and sometimes even going back to the site if I can’t get comfortable with it.\n\nSo maybe not “control every aspect,” exactly, but I do feel like I need enough control over the job to be sure I didn’t miss anything. And lately it’s been taking longer than it should. People are starting to notice that, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work has to be perfect in order for you to consider it complete?\n\nPatient: Perfect might be too strong a word… I don’t want to make it sound like I’m impossible to satisfy.\n\nBut yeah, I guess in practice I don’t really let myself call it done until I’ve checked the important things again—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took. And sometimes I’ll keep going back over the same stuff because I’m worried I missed something.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say, but a simple install can end up taking way longer than it should. And there have been times I’ve driven back to a site just to be sure. People are starting to notice, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the way you check things has to follow a specific routine or pattern to feel complete?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there is a routine to it, though I don’t know if I’d call it a pattern exactly.\n\nI’ll usually go back through the same things—breakers, labels, torque marks, then the photos I took—and I kind of need to do it in that way before I feel okay saying it’s done. When I’m checking, it’s more about making sure it’s safe and correct, not so much that it feels “just right.”\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what you mean by that?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to control every aspect of your work to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say every aspect… I mean, I don’t want to sound like I’m trying to micromanage everything.\n\nBut with my own work, yeah, I do feel like I can’t really relax until I’ve gone back through the important parts again—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’m just being thorough, which… in this line of work isn’t a bad thing. But then it turns into checking the same things more than once, and sometimes even going back to the site if I can’t get comfortable with it.\n\nSo maybe not “control every aspect,” exactly, but I do feel like I need enough control over the job to be sure I didn’t miss anything. And lately it’s been taking longer than it should. People are starting to notice that, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work has to be perfect in order for you to consider it complete?\n\nPatient: Perfect might be too strong a word… I don’t want to make it sound like I’m impossible to satisfy.\n\nBut yeah, I guess in practice I don’t really let myself call it done until I’ve checked the important things again—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took. And sometimes I’ll keep going back over the same stuff because I’m worried I missed something.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say, but a simple install can end up taking way longer than it should. And there have been times I’ve driven back to a site just to be sure. People are starting to notice, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the way you check things has to follow a specific routine or pattern to feel complete?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there is a routine to it, though I don’t know if I’d call it a pattern exactly.\n\nI’ll usually go back through the same things—breakers, labels, torque marks, then the photos I took—and I kind of need to do it in that way before I feel okay saying it’s done. It’s not like a ritual in my head, or at least I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but if I skip something or feel like I rushed it, I’ll start thinking maybe I missed something.\n\nAnd then I end up checking again… sometimes more than once. A simple install can take a lot longer than it should, and I’ve even driven back to a site before just to make sure. I know that probably sounds excessive. People are starting to notice, I think.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your work, is it more about preventing something bad from happening or making sure everything is just right?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about preventing something bad from happening… though I tell myself it’s just making sure everything is right.\n\nWith electrical work, if I start wondering whether I missed a breaker label or a torque mark or something in the photos, I get this feeling like I shouldn’t leave it alone yet. So I go back over it again. And sometimes again after that.\n\nI don’t mean that I’m trying to be difficult or waste time. I know being careful is part of the job. But lately it’s gone past normal careful, I think. A simple install can take me way longer than it should, and there have been times I’ve driven back to a site just to check. I haven’t noticed it causing the same kind of problem at home. It’s really the job stuff that’s gotten embarrassing, because a straightforward install can take me way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind and are difficult to get rid of?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s constant, exactly, but there are definitely thoughts that kind of barge in and won’t leave.\n\nLike even after I know I checked a panel or a disconnect, I’ll get this thought of, “What if I missed one loose neutral?” or “What if I left something that could start a fire?” And I’ll try to tell myself I already checked it, that it’s fine, but it can follow me all the way home.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not trying to be difficult or anything—it just gets hard to shake once it’s in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about missing something or causing a fire make sense, or do they seem irrational to you?\n\nPatient: Part of me knows it’s probably more than it needs to be. I mean, I do check the panel or the disconnect, and I’m not careless at work.\n\nBut then the thought barges in anyway—like, what if I missed one loose neutral, or what if I left something that could start a fire? And even if I try to shake it off, it can follow me all the way home.\n\nSo… I guess it doesn’t feel completely rational, but it feels hard to ignore, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah. I don’t know if it’s a strict “order” exactly, but I feel like I have to go back over the same things until it feels settled enough to leave it alone.\n\nSo I’ll check the breakers, the labels, the torque marks, then I’ll look at the photos again… and if something doesn’t feel right, I may start over or keep circling back. Sometimes I even drive back to the site, which I know sounds like a lot.\n\nIt’s not that I want to waste time. It’s just that a simple install ends up taking way longer than it should, and, honestly, people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking things to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to overstate it, but it does feel like I’m checking so nothing gets missed that could turn into a problem.\n\nThat’s kind of what brings me in, honestly. I keep going back over jobs—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took—and sometimes I’ll even drive back to the site if I can’t let it go. I know I’m trying to prevent something bad from happening, but it gets to where a simple install takes way longer than it should.\n\nAnd, uh… people are starting to notice, so I figured I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors and thoughts affecting your relationships with family or friends outside of work?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s ruining things or anything… but it does spill over.\n\nMy schedule has been slipping because jobs I should wrap up in one visit turn into me calling back, checking again, looking over pictures, that kind of thing. And that takes time away from the apprentices too, because I’m not as available or I’m tied up going back over something.\n\nOutside of work, I’ve been skipping some stuff I used to do after hours—seeing people, doing things I enjoy—because I’m still reviewing a job in my head or wondering if I need to go back. So… yeah, I guess it’s affecting things more than I’d like to admit. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors and thoughts are significantly interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if I’m being honest, it is starting to.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but my schedule’s been slipping because jobs that should be done in one visit turn into callbacks, extra checks, looking things over again. And that’s starting to affect how confident clients are in me, I think… plus it takes time away from the apprentices when I’m tied up rechecking instead of being available.\n\nAnd after work, I’ve been skipping things I used to enjoy because I’m still stuck reviewing a job in my head or wondering if I need to go back. So… yeah, I’d say it’s interfering more than I’d like. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts and checking behaviors cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… not unbearable all the time, but it’s definitely stressful.\n\nWhat brings me in is I keep going back over jobs again and again—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took—just trying to be sure I didn’t miss something. And sometimes I’ll even drive back to a site if I can’t shake it.\n\nSo the distress is more like… I know it shouldn’t take over the day like that, but it does. A simple install ends up taking way longer than it should, and then I feel embarrassed because people are starting to notice. I don’t know if that’s “significant” enough, but it feels like more than I can keep brushing off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be just right or symmetrical when you're checking your work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really symmetrical, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s not like things have to line up a certain way or look perfect for the sake of it. I mean, I like my work neat, of course, but I think that’s pretty normal in electrical work. When I’m checking, it’s more about making sure it’s safe and correct, not so much that it feels “just right.”\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what you mean by that?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to control every aspect of your work to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say every aspect… I mean, I don’t want to sound like I’m trying to micromanage everything.\n\nBut with my own work, yeah, I do feel like I can’t really relax until I’ve gone back through the important parts again—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’m just being thorough, which… in this line of work isn’t a bad thing. But then it turns into checking the same things more than once, and sometimes even going back to the site if I can’t get comfortable with it.\n\nSo maybe not “control every aspect,” exactly, but I do feel like I need enough control over the job to be sure I didn’t miss anything. And lately it’s been taking longer than it should. People are starting to notice that, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work has to be perfect in order for you to consider it complete?\n\nPatient: Perfect might be too strong a word… I don’t want to make it sound like I’m impossible to satisfy.\n\nBut yeah, I guess in practice I don’t really let myself call it done until I’ve checked the important things again—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took. And sometimes I’ll keep going back over the same stuff because I’m worried I missed something.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say, but a simple install can end up taking way longer than it should. And there have been times I’ve driven back to a site just to be sure. People are starting to notice, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the way you check things has to follow a specific routine or pattern to feel complete?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there is a routine to it, though I don’t know if I’d call it a pattern exactly.\n\nI’ll usually go back through the same things—breakers, labels, torque marks, then the photos I took—and I kind of need to do it in that way before I feel okay saying it’s done. It’s not like a ritual in my head, or at least I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but if I skip something or feel like I rushed it, I’ll start thinking maybe I missed something.\n\nAnd then I end up checking again… sometimes more than once. A simple install can take a lot longer than it should, and I’ve even driven back to a site before just to make sure. I know that probably sounds excessive. People are starting to notice, I think.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your work, is it more about preventing something bad from happening or making sure everything is just right?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about preventing something bad from happening… though I tell myself it’s just making sure everything is right.\n\nWith electrical work, if I start wondering whether I missed a breaker label or a torque mark or something in the photos, I get this feeling like I shouldn’t leave it alone yet. So I go back over it again. And sometimes again after that.\n\nI don’t mean that I’m trying to be difficult or waste time. I know being careful is part of the job. But lately it’s gone past normal careful, I think. A simple install can take me way longer than it should, and there have been times I’ve driven back to a site just to check. People are starting to notice, which is part of why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be in a specific order or have a certain arrangement to feel right?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s so much an “arrangement,” like things have to be lined up a certain way. It’s more that I end up going back through the same set of checks—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos—and I feel better if I’ve covered them all.\n\nI’m not trying to make it sound more serious than it is, but what brings me in is that I keep going back over jobs again and again. Sometimes even after I’ve left, I’ll drive back to the site just to be sure. And then a simple install takes way longer than it should… and people are starting to notice. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It’s really the job stuff that’s gotten embarrassing, because a straightforward install can take me way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind and are difficult to get rid of?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s constant, exactly, but there are definitely thoughts that kind of barge in and won’t leave.\n\nLike even after I know I checked a panel or a disconnect, I’ll get this thought of, “What if I missed one loose neutral?” or “What if I left something that could start a fire?” And I’ll try to tell myself I already checked it, that it’s fine, but it can follow me all the way home.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not trying to be difficult or anything—it just gets hard to shake once it’s in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about missing something or causing a fire make sense, or do they seem irrational to you?\n\nPatient: Part of me knows it’s probably more than it needs to be. I mean, I do check the panel or the disconnect, and I’m not careless at work.\n\nBut then the thought barges in anyway—like, what if I missed one loose neutral, or what if I left something that could start a fire? And even if I try to shake it off, it can follow me all the way home.\n\nSo… I guess it doesn’t feel completely rational, but it feels hard to ignore, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah. I don’t know if it’s a strict “order” exactly, but I feel like I have to go back over the same things until it feels settled enough to leave it alone.\n\nSo I’ll check the breakers, the labels, the torque marks, then I’ll look at the photos again… and if something doesn’t feel right, I may start over or keep circling back. Sometimes I even drive back to the site, which I know sounds like a lot.\n\nIt’s not that I want to waste time. It’s just that a simple install ends up taking way longer than it should, and, honestly, people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking things to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to overstate it, but it does feel like I’m checking so nothing gets missed that could turn into a problem.\n\nThat’s kind of what brings me in, honestly. I keep going back over jobs—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took—and sometimes I’ll even drive back to the site if I can’t let it go. I know I’m trying to prevent something bad from happening, but it gets to where a simple install takes way longer than it should.\n\nAnd, uh… people are starting to notice, so I figured I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors and thoughts affecting your relationships with family or friends outside of work?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s ruining things or anything… but it does spill over.\n\nMy schedule has been slipping because jobs I should wrap up in one visit turn into me calling back, checking again, looking over pictures, that kind of thing. And that takes time away from the apprentices too, because I’m not as available or I’m tied up going back over something.\n\nOutside of work, I’ve been skipping some stuff I used to do after hours—seeing people, doing things I enjoy—because I’m still reviewing a job in my head or wondering if I need to go back. So… yeah, I guess it’s affecting things more than I’d like to admit. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors and thoughts are significantly interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if I’m being honest, it is starting to.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but my schedule’s been slipping because jobs that should be done in one visit turn into callbacks, extra checks, looking things over again. And that’s starting to affect how confident clients are in me, I think… plus it takes time away from the apprentices when I’m tied up rechecking instead of being available.\n\nAnd after work, I’ve been skipping things I used to enjoy because I’m still stuck reviewing a job in my head or wondering if I need to go back. So… yeah, I’d say it’s interfering more than I’d like. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts and checking behaviors cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… not unbearable all the time, but it’s definitely stressful.\n\nWhat brings me in is I keep going back over jobs again and again—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took—just trying to be sure I didn’t miss something. And sometimes I’ll even drive back to a site if I can’t shake it.\n\nSo the distress is more like… I know it shouldn’t take over the day like that, but it does. A simple install ends up taking way longer than it should, and then I feel embarrassed because people are starting to notice. I don’t know if that’s “significant” enough, but it feels like more than I can keep brushing off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be just right or symmetrical when you're checking your work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really symmetrical, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s not like things have to line up a certain way or look perfect for the sake of it. I mean, I like my work neat, of course, but I think that’s pretty normal in electrical work. When I’m checking, it’s more about making sure it’s safe and correct, not so much that it feels “just right.”\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what you mean by that?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to control every aspect of your work to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say every aspect… I mean, I don’t want to sound like I’m trying to micromanage everything.\n\nBut with my own work, yeah, I do feel like I can’t really relax until I’ve gone back through the important parts again—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’m just being thorough, which… in this line of work isn’t a bad thing. But then it turns into checking the same things more than once, and sometimes even going back to the site if I can’t get comfortable with it.\n\nSo maybe not “control every aspect,” exactly, but I do feel like I need enough control over the job to be sure I didn’t miss anything. And lately it’s been taking longer than it should. People are starting to notice that, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work has to be perfect in order for you to consider it complete?\n\nPatient: Perfect might be too strong a word… I don’t want to make it sound like I’m impossible to satisfy.\n\nBut yeah, I guess in practice I don’t really let myself call it done until I’ve checked the important things again—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took. And sometimes I’ll keep going back over the same stuff because I’m worried I missed something.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say, but a simple install can end up taking way longer than it should. And there have been times I’ve driven back to a site just to be sure. People are starting to notice, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the way you check things has to follow a specific routine or pattern to feel complete?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there is a routine to it, though I don’t know if I’d call it a pattern exactly.\n\nI’ll usually go back through the same things—breakers, labels, torque marks, then the photos I took—and I kind of need to do it in that way before I feel okay saying it’s done. It’s not like a ritual in my head, or at least I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but if I skip something or feel like I rushed it, I’ll start thinking maybe I missed something.\n\nAnd then I end up checking again… sometimes more than once. A simple install can take a lot longer than it should, and I’ve even driven back to a site before just to make sure. I know that probably sounds excessive. People are starting to notice, I think.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your work, is it more about preventing something bad from happening or making sure everything is just right?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about preventing something bad from happening… though I tell myself it’s just making sure everything is right.\n\nWith electrical work, if I start wondering whether I missed a breaker label or a torque mark or something in the photos, I get this feeling like I shouldn’t leave it alone yet. So I go back over it again. And sometimes again after that.\n\nI don’t mean that I’m trying to be difficult or waste time. I know being careful is part of the job. But lately it’s gone past normal careful, I think. A simple install can take me way longer than it should, and there have been times I’ve driven back to a site just to check. People are starting to notice, which is part of why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be in a specific order or have a certain arrangement to feel right?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s so much an “arrangement,” like things have to be lined up a certain way. It’s more that I end up going back through the same set of checks—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos—and I feel better if I’ve covered them all.\n\nI’m not trying to make it sound more serious than it is, but what brings me in is that I keep going back over jobs again and again. Sometimes even after I’ve left, I’ll drive back to the site just to be sure. And then a simple install takes way longer than it should… and people are starting to notice. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to have a certain level of precision or exactness to feel right?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, to a point. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m being obsessive about every tiny thing, but with work stuff, it does feel like it has to be exact enough before I can let it go.\n\nLike I’ll keep going back over breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took… and if something doesn’t feel quite confirmed, I’ll check again. Sometimes I’ve even driven back to a site, which I know probably sounds like a lot. That’s really what brought me in—simple installs are taking way longer than they should, and people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be symmetrical or have a specific arrangement to feel right?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I don’t think it’s about symmetry or having things arranged a certain way. I haven’t noticed myself needing things lined up or balanced or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more just… whether I can feel comfortable that I checked what I was supposed to check. If that answers what you’re asking.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be in a specific order or have a certain arrangement to feel right?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s so much an “arrangement,” like things have to be lined up a certain way. It’s more that I end up going back through the same set of checks—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos—and I feel better if I’ve covered them all.\n\nI’m not trying to make it sound more serious than it is, but what brings me in is that I keep going back over jobs again and again. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m being obsessive about every tiny thing, but with work stuff, it does feel like it has to be exact enough before I can let it go.\n\nLike I’ll keep going back over breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took… and if something doesn’t feel quite confirmed, I’ll check again. Sometimes I’ve even driven back to a site, which I know probably sounds like a lot. That’s really what brought me in—simple installs are taking way longer than they should, and people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be symmetrical or have a specific arrangement to feel right?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I don’t think it’s about symmetry or having things arranged a certain way. I haven’t noticed myself needing things lined up or balanced or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more just… whether I can feel comfortable that I checked what I was supposed to check. If that answers what you’re asking.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like the precision or exactness of your work is what's driving you to check things repeatedly?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 45 / electrician / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compulsions\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is I keep going back over jobs again and again—checking breakers, labels, torque marks, photos, even driving back to a site sometimes—until a simple install takes way longer than it should and people are starting to notice.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think that’s probably a big part of it. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m trying to be perfect just for the sake of it, but with the work, if I don’t feel sure enough, I have a hard time leaving it alone.

So I’ll go back over the breakers, the labels, the torque marks, the photos… sometimes more than once. And, yeah, there have been times I’ve driven back to a site after leaving, just to make sure. It’s embarrassing to say, but that’s kind of why I’m here — jobs that should be straightforward end up taking way longer, and people are starting to notice.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s mostly work stuff that’s gotten a little out of hand. I’m an electrician, and I keep finding myself going back over jobs again and again—checking breakers, labels, torque marks, photos, things like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll even drive back to a site just to make sure I didn’t miss something. I mean, I want to do good work, obviously, but a simple install is taking me way longer than it should, and people are starting to notice. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but… that’s what’s been bringing me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating these checking behaviors in other areas of your life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s at work, honestly. That’s where it shows up the clearest—like I’ll check the breakers and labels and torque marks, look back through photos, and sometimes even go back to the site if I can’t shake the feeling I missed something.\n\nOutside of work… I mean, not in a way I’d want to make sound dramatic. I haven’t noticed it causing the same kind of problem at home. It’s really the job stuff that’s gotten embarrassing, because a straightforward install can take me way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind and are difficult to get rid of?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s constant, exactly, but there are definitely thoughts that kind of barge in and won’t leave.\n\nLike even after I know I checked a panel or a disconnect, I’ll get this thought of, “What if I missed one loose neutral?” or “What if I left something that could start a fire?” And I’ll try to tell myself I already checked it, that it’s fine, but it can follow me all the way home.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not trying to be difficult or anything—it just gets hard to shake once it’s in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about missing something or causing a fire make sense, or do they seem irrational to you?\n\nPatient: Part of me knows it’s probably more than it needs to be. I mean, I do check the panel or the disconnect, and I’m not careless at work.\n\nBut then the thought barges in anyway—like, what if I missed one loose neutral, or what if I left something that could start a fire? And even if I try to shake it off, it can follow me all the way home.\n\nSo… I guess it doesn’t feel completely rational, but it feels hard to ignore, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah. I don’t know if it’s a strict “order” exactly, but I feel like I have to go back over the same things until it feels settled enough to leave it alone.\n\nSo I’ll check the breakers, the labels, the torque marks, then I’ll look at the photos again… and if something doesn’t feel right, I may start over or keep circling back. Sometimes I even drive back to the site, which I know sounds like a lot.\n\nIt’s not that I want to waste time. It’s just that a simple install ends up taking way longer than it should, and, honestly, people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking things to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to overstate it, but it does feel like I’m checking so nothing gets missed that could turn into a problem.\n\nThat’s kind of what brings me in, honestly. I keep going back over jobs—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took—and sometimes I’ll even drive back to the site if I can’t let it go. I know I’m trying to prevent something bad from happening, but it gets to where a simple install takes way longer than it should.\n\nAnd, uh… people are starting to notice, so I figured I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors and thoughts affecting your relationships with family or friends outside of work?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s ruining things or anything… but it does spill over.\n\nMy schedule has been slipping because jobs I should wrap up in one visit turn into me calling back, checking again, looking over pictures, that kind of thing. And that takes time away from the apprentices too, because I’m not as available or I’m tied up going back over something.\n\nOutside of work, I’ve been skipping some stuff I used to do after hours—seeing people, doing things I enjoy—because I’m still reviewing a job in my head or wondering if I need to go back. So… yeah, I guess it’s affecting things more than I’d like to admit. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors and thoughts are significantly interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if I’m being honest, it is starting to.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but my schedule’s been slipping because jobs that should be done in one visit turn into callbacks, extra checks, looking things over again. And that’s starting to affect how confident clients are in me, I think… plus it takes time away from the apprentices when I’m tied up rechecking instead of being available.\n\nAnd after work, I’ve been skipping things I used to enjoy because I’m still stuck reviewing a job in my head or wondering if I need to go back. So… yeah, I’d say it’s interfering more than I’d like. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts and checking behaviors cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… not unbearable all the time, but it’s definitely stressful.\n\nWhat brings me in is I keep going back over jobs again and again—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took—just trying to be sure I didn’t miss something. And sometimes I’ll even drive back to a site if I can’t shake it.\n\nSo the distress is more like… I know it shouldn’t take over the day like that, but it does. A simple install ends up taking way longer than it should, and then I feel embarrassed because people are starting to notice. I don’t know if that’s “significant” enough, but it feels like more than I can keep brushing off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be just right or symmetrical when you're checking your work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really symmetrical, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s not like things have to line up a certain way or look perfect for the sake of it. I mean, I like my work neat, of course, but I think that’s pretty normal in electrical work. When I’m checking, it’s more about making sure it’s safe and correct, not so much that it feels “just right.”\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what you mean by that?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to control every aspect of your work to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say every aspect… I mean, I don’t want to sound like I’m trying to micromanage everything.\n\nBut with my own work, yeah, I do feel like I can’t really relax until I’ve gone back through the important parts again—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’m just being thorough, which… in this line of work isn’t a bad thing. But then it turns into checking the same things more than once, and sometimes even going back to the site if I can’t get comfortable with it.\n\nSo maybe not “control every aspect,” exactly, but I do feel like I need enough control over the job to be sure I didn’t miss anything. And lately it’s been taking longer than it should. People are starting to notice that, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work has to be perfect in order for you to consider it complete?\n\nPatient: Perfect might be too strong a word… I don’t want to make it sound like I’m impossible to satisfy.\n\nBut yeah, I guess in practice I don’t really let myself call it done until I’ve checked the important things again—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took. And sometimes I’ll keep going back over the same stuff because I’m worried I missed something.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say, but a simple install can end up taking way longer than it should. And there have been times I’ve driven back to a site just to be sure. People are starting to notice, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the way you check things has to follow a specific routine or pattern to feel complete?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there is a routine to it, though I don’t know if I’d call it a pattern exactly.\n\nI’ll usually go back through the same things—breakers, labels, torque marks, then the photos I took—and I kind of need to do it in that way before I feel okay saying it’s done. It’s not like a ritual in my head, or at least I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but if I skip something or feel like I rushed it, I’ll start thinking maybe I missed something.\n\nAnd then I end up checking again… sometimes more than once. A simple install can take a lot longer than it should, and I’ve even driven back to a site before just to make sure. I know that probably sounds excessive. People are starting to notice, I think.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your work, is it more about preventing something bad from happening or making sure everything is just right?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about preventing something bad from happening… though I tell myself it’s just making sure everything is right.\n\nWith electrical work, if I start wondering whether I missed a breaker label or a torque mark or something in the photos, I get this feeling like I shouldn’t leave it alone yet. So I go back over it again. And sometimes again after that.\n\nI don’t mean that I’m trying to be difficult or waste time. I know being careful is part of the job. But lately it’s gone past normal careful, I think. A simple install can take me way longer than it should, and there have been times I’ve driven back to a site just to check. People are starting to notice, which is part of why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be in a specific order or have a certain arrangement to feel right?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s so much an “arrangement,” like things have to be lined up a certain way. It’s more that I end up going back through the same set of checks—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos—and I feel better if I’ve covered them all.\n\nI’m not trying to make it sound more serious than it is, but what brings me in is that I keep going back over jobs again and again. Sometimes even after I’ve left, I’ll drive back to the site just to be sure. And then a simple install takes way longer than it should… and people are starting to notice. Sometimes I’ve even driven back to a site, which I know probably sounds like a lot. Not so much symmetrical, no. I don’t really care if things look balanced or lined up in some particular way.

It’s more… I need to feel like the job is exactly right, or at least that I’ve confirmed it enough. I don’t know if it’s a strict “order” exactly, but I feel like I have to go back over the same things until it feels settled enough to leave it alone.\n\nSo I’ll check the breakers, the labels, the torque marks, then I’ll look at the photos again… and if something doesn’t feel right, I may start over or keep circling back.
